\mathcal{A}G=\displaystyle G-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{i}y_{i}x_{i}^{*}}{|h_{i}|^{2}}.

The proof will be by induction on
r,
which is the least index
j\geq 0
such that
T_{j}=0,
where
T_{0}=H_{G},T_{1},T_{2},\dots
is a sequence of operators recursively generated by the algorithm.

Step 0.

Let
t_{0}=\|H_{G}\|.

If
t_{0}=0,
then
H_{G}=0,
which implies
G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}).
In this case, the algorithm terminates, we define
r
to be zero and the superoptimal approximant
\mathcal{A}G
is given by
\mathcal{A}G=G
, in agreement with the formula

G-\mathcal{A}G=\sum\limits_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{j}y_{j}x_{j}^{*}}{x_{j}^{*}x_{j}}

(4.16)

contained in the statement of Theorem
10.15
(since the sum on the right hand side of equation (
4.16
) is empty, and therefore by convention is interpreted as being zero).

Otherwise,
t_{0}>0
. By Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
4.12
,
H_{G}
is a compact operator and so there exists a Schmidt pair
(x_{0},y_{0})
corresponding to the singular value
t_{0}
of
H_{G}.
By the definition of the Schmidt pair
(x_{0},y_{0})
corresponding to
t_{0}
for the Hankel operator

H_{G}:H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp},

x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad y_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

are non-zero vector-valued functions such that

H_{G}x_{0}=t_{0}y_{0}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad H_{G}^{*}y_{0}=t_{0}x_{0}.

By Lemma
4.12
,
x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
admit inner-outer factorizations

x_{0}=\xi_{0}h_{0},\quad\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}=\eta_{0}h_{0}

(4.17)

for some scalar outer factor
h_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
and column matrix inner functions
\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
,
\eta_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).
Then

\|x_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=|h_{0}(z)|=\|y_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\;\text{almost everywhere on }\;\mathbb{T}.

(4.18)

We write equations (
4.17
) as

\xi_{0}=\frac{x_{0}}{h_{0}}\;,\;\eta_{0}=\frac{\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}}{h_{0}}.

(4.19)

By equations (
4.18
) and (
4.19
),

\|\xi_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1=\|\eta_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(4.20)

Let
t_{0}\neq 0.
By Lemma
4.12
, every function
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
which is at minimal distance from
G
satisfies

(G-Q_{1})x_{0}=t_{0}y_{0},\quad y_{0}^{*}(G-Q_{1})=t_{0}x_{0}^{*}.

(4.21)

Step 1.
Let

X_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\vskip 8.61108pt.

(4.22)

By Proposition
6.1
,

X_{1}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
Similarly,

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\subset zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})

and therefore

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\overline{zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})}\subset\bar{z}\overline{H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})},

that is, if

Y_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp},

(4.23)

then

Y_{1}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

By Proposition
7.1
,

Y_{1}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Choose any function
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
which satisfies the equations (
4.21
). Consider the operator
T_{1}:X_{1}\to Y_{1}
defined by

T_{1}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)\;\text{for all}\;x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

(4.24)

where
P_{Y_{1}}
is the projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
on
Y_{1}.

By Corollary
7.2
and Proposition
8.1
,
T_{1}
is well-defined.

If
T_{1}=0,
then the algorithm terminates, we define
r
to be
1
and, in agreement with Theorem
10.15
, the superoptimal approximant
\mathcal{A}G
is given by the formula

G-\mathcal{A}G=\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{i}y_{i}x_{i}^{*}}{|h_{i}|^{2}}=\frac{t_{0}y_{0}x_{0}^{*}}{|h_{0}|^{2}},

and the solution is

\mathcal{A}G=G-\frac{t_{0}y_{0}x_{0}^{*}}{|h_{0}|^{2}}.

If
T_{1}\neq 0,
let
t_{1}=\|T_{1}\|>0.
By Theorem
9.1
,
T_{1}
is a compact operator and so
there exist
v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\;w_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}w_{1})
is a Schmidt pair for
T_{1}
corresponding to
t_{1}.
Let
h_{1}
be the scalar outer factor of

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}
and let

x_{1}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*})v_{1},\;\;y_{1}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T})w_{1},

(4.25)

where
\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
and
\mathrm{I}_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
are the identity operators in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}^{m}
respectively.
Then, by Proposition
5.1
,

\|x_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=|h_{1}(z)|=\|y_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(4.26)

By Theorem
1.3
, there exists a function
Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
such that both

s_{0}^{\infty}(G-Q_{2})
and
s_{1}^{\infty}(G-Q_{2})
are minimized and

s_{1}^{\infty}(G-Q_{2})=t_{1}.

By Proposition
9.16
, any such
Q_{2}
satisfies

\displaystyle(G-Q_{2})x_{0}=t_{0}y_{0},\quad y_{0}^{*}(G-Q_{2})=t_{0}x_{0}^{*}

(4.27)

\displaystyle(G-Q_{2})x_{1}=t_{1}y_{1},\quad y_{1}^{*}(G-Q_{2})=t_{1}x_{1}^{*}.

Choose any function
Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
which satisfies the equations (
4.27
).

Define

\xi_{1}=\frac{x_{1}}{h_{1}},\quad\eta_{1}=\frac{\bar{z}\bar{y}_{1}}{h_{1}}.

(4.28)

By equations (
4.26
) and (
4.28
),
\|\xi_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1=\|\eta_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Step 2.
Define

\begin{array}[]{cllll}X_{2}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\\
Y_{2}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.\end{array}

Note that, by Proposition
6.2
,
X_{2}
is a closed linear subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{n}),
and, by Proposition
7.3
,
Y_{2}
is a closed linear subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Now consider the operator
T_{2}:X_{2}\to Y_{2}
given by

T_{2}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{2}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{2})x),

(4.29)

where
P_{Y_{2}}
is the projection from
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
on
Y_{2}.

By Corollary
7.4
and Proposition
8.1
,
T_{2}
is well defined, that is, it does not depend on the choice of
Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfying equations (
4.27
).
If
T_{2}=0,
then the algorithm terminates, we define
r
to be
2
and, according to Theorem
10.15
, the superoptimal approximant
\mathcal{A}G
is given by the formula

G-\mathcal{A}G=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{i}y_{i}x_{i}^{*}}{|h_{i}|^{2}}=\frac{t_{0}y_{0}x_{0}^{*}}{|h_{0}|^{2}}+\frac{t_{1}y_{1}x_{1}^{*}}{|h_{1}|^{2}}.

If
T_{2}\neq 0
, then let
t_{2}=\|T_{2}\|.
By Theorem
9.26
,
T_{2}
is a compact operator and hence there exist
v_{2}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\;w_{2}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}v_{2},\;\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}w_{2})

is a Schmidt pair for
T_{2}
corresponding to
\|T_{2}\|=t_{2}.

Let
h_{2}
be the scalar outer factor of

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}v_{2}
.
Note that, by Proposition
6.2
,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}v_{2}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Let

x_{2}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\xi_{1}\xi_{1}^{*})v_{2},\;\;y_{2}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}-\bar{\eta}_{1}\eta_{1}^{T})w_{2}.

(4.30)

Then, by Proposition
5.1
,

\|x_{2}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=|h_{2}(z)|=\|y_{2}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(4.31)

Define

\xi_{2}=\frac{x_{2}}{h_{2}},\;\eta_{2}=\frac{\bar{z}\bar{y}_{2}}{h_{2}}.

(4.32)

Clearly
\|\xi_{2}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1
and
\|\eta_{2}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Recursive step.
Suppose that, for
j\leq\min(m,n)-2
, we have constructed

\displaystyle t_{0}\geq t_{1}\geq\cdots\geq t_{j}>0

(4.33)

\displaystyle x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{j}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})

\displaystyle y_{0},y_{1},\cdots,y_{j}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})

\displaystyle h_{0},h_{1},\cdots,h_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})\;\text{outer}

\displaystyle\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})\;\text{pointwise orthonormal on}\;\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\cdots,\eta_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})\;\text{pointwise orthonormal on}\;\mathbb{T}

\displaystyle X_{0}=H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),X_{1},\cdots,X_{j}

\displaystyle Y_{0}=H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp},Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{j}

\displaystyle T_{0},T_{1},\cdots,T_{j}\;\text{compact operators}.

By Theorem
1.3
, there exists a function
Q_{j+1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
such that

\left(s_{0}^{\infty}(G-Q_{j+1}),s_{1}^{\infty}(G-Q_{j+1}),\cdots,s_{j+1}^{\infty}(G-Q_{j+1})\right)

is lexicographically minimized. By Proposition
10.5
, any such function
Q_{j+1}
satisfies

(G-Q_{j+1})x_{i}=t_{i}y_{i},\quad y_{i}^{*}(G-Q_{j+1})=t_{i}x_{i}^{*},\quad i=0,1,\cdots,j.

(4.34)

Choose any function
Q_{j+1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
which satisfies the equations (
4.34
). Define

X_{j+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})

(4.35)

Y_{j+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

(4.36)

Note that, by Proposition
6.1
,
X_{j+1}
is a subset of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}),
and, by Proposition
10.8
,
Y_{j+1}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Consider the operator

T_{j+1}:X_{j+1}\to Y_{j+1}

given by

T_{j+1}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{j+1}}\left(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j+1})x\right).

(4.37)

By Corollary
7.4
and Proposition
8.1
,
T_{j+1}
is well-defined and does not depend on the choice of
Q_{j+1}
subject to equations (
4.34
).

If
T_{j+1}=0,
then the algorithm terminates, we define
r
to be
j+1,
and, according to Theorem
10.15
, the superoptimal approximant
\mathcal{A}G
is given by the formula

G-\mathcal{A}G=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{i}y_{i}x_{i}^{*}}{|h_{i}|^{2}}.

Otherwise, we define
t_{j+1}=\|T_{j+1}\|>0.
By Theorem
9.1
,
T_{j+1}
is a compact operator and hence there exist
v_{j+1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\;w_{j+1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}v_{j+1},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}w_{j+1})

(4.38)

is a Schmidt pair for
T_{j+1}
corresponding to the singular value
t_{j+1}.

Let
h_{j+1}
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}v_{j+1},
and let

x_{j+1}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\cdots-\xi_{j}\xi_{j}^{*})v_{j+1},\quad y_{j+1}=(I_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}-\cdots-\bar{\eta}_{j}\eta_{j}^{T})w_{j+1}.

(4.39)

Then, by Proposition
5.1
,

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$x_{j}$}}}{\@index{\@indexphrase{$y_{j}$}}}\|x_{j+1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=|h_{j+1}(z)|=\|y_{j+1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(4.40)

We define

\xi_{j+1}=\frac{x_{j+1}}{h_{j+1}},\quad\eta_{j+1}=\frac{\bar{z}\overline{y_{j+1}}}{h_{j+1}}.

(4.41)

Clearly
\|\xi_{j+1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1
and
\|\eta_{j+1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

This completes the recursive step. The algorithm terminates after at most
\min(m,n)
steps, so that
r\leq\min(m,n)
and, in accordance with Theorem
10.15
the superoptimal approximant
\mathcal{A}G
is given by the formula

G-\mathcal{A}G=\sum\limits_{i=0}^{r-1}\frac{t_{i}y_{i}x_{i}^{*}}{|h_{i}|^{2}}.

∎

5.
Pointwise orthonormality of
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{j}
and
\{\bar{\eta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{j}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}

These orthonormality properties will be needed for the justification of the main algorithm.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
m,n
be positive integers with
\min(m,n)\geq 2,
let

G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
and let
0\leq j\leq\min(m,n)-2.
Suppose we have applied steps
0,\dots,j
of the superoptimal analytic approximation algorithm from Subsection
4.2
to
G
and we have obtained
x_{i},y_{i}
as in equations (
4.39
), and
\xi_{i},\eta_{i}
as in equations (
4.41
) for
i=0,\cdots,j.

Then

(i)

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x_{1},\quad\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}x_{j},

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}w_{1}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}y_{1},

and
\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}y_{j};

(ii)

\|x_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|y_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=|h_{j}(z)|
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T};

(iii)

The sets
\{\xi_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j}
and
\{\bar{\eta}_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j}
are orthonormal in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathbb{C}^{m}
respectively for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T}.

Proof.

We will prove statement (ii) in Propositions
9.13
and
9.29
. Statement (i) is proven below in equations (
5.5
), (
5.9
), (
5
).
Let us prove assertion (iii).

Since the function
G
belongs to
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}),
by Hartman’s theorem, the Hankel operator with symbol
G
, denoted by
H_{G},
is a compact operator, and so there exist functions

x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad y_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

such that
(x_{0},y_{0})
is a Schmidt pair corresponding to the singular value
t_{0}=\|H_{G}\|\neq 0.
By Lemma
4.12
,
x_{0},\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}
admit the inner-outer factorizations

x_{0}=\xi_{0}h_{0},\quad\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}=\eta_{0}h_{0}

for column matrix inner functions
\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
,
\eta_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and some scalar outer factor
h_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}).
By Theorem
4.11
,

\|x_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=|h_{0}(z)|=\|y_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;\;\text{almost everywhere on }\;\mathbb{T}.

(5.1)

Thus

\|\xi_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(5.2)

Hence (iii) of Proposition
5.1
holds for
\{\xi_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j}
in the case that
j=0.

Let
T_{1}
be given by equation (
4.24
). By the hypothesis (
4.33
),
T_{1}
is a compact operator, and if
T_{1}\neq 0,
then there exist
v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
w_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that
(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}w_{1})
is a Schmidt pair corresponding to
\|T_{1}\|=t_{1}.
By Proposition
3.24
,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Let
h_{1}
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}.
We define

x_{1}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*})v_{1}

(5.3)

and

\xi_{1}=\frac{x_{1}}{h_{1}}.

(5.4)

Then, for
z\in\mathbb{D},

\xi_{1}(z)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}v_{1}(z)-\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z).

Note that by equation (
5.2
),

\xi_{0}^{*}(z)\xi_{0}(z)=\langle\xi_{0}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T},

hence

\langle\xi_{1}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\xi_{0}^{*}(z)\xi_{1}(z)=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z)-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)^{*}\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z)=0

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Note that, by equation (
5.3
), for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T},

\displaystyle\xi_{0}(z)\wedge v_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge(x_{1}(z)+\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z))\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge x_{1}(z)+\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z))\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge x_{1}(z),

(5.5)

the last equality following from the pointwise linear dependence of the vectors
\xi_{0}
and

z\mapsto\xi_{0}(z)\langle v_{1}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Moreover,
since
h_{1}
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1},
for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T},
we have

\begin{array}[]{clll}|h_{1}(z)|&=\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge v_{1}(z)\|_{\wedge^{2}{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}=\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge x_{1}(z)\|_{\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}},\end{array}

By Lemma
3.14
,

\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge x_{1}(z)\|_{\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|x_{1}(z)-\langle x_{1}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|x_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Hence, for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T},

|h_{1}(z)|=\|x_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

(5.6)

and thus

\|\xi_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\displaystyle\frac{\|x_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}{|h_{1}(z)|}=1\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

Consequently,
\{\xi_{0}(z),\xi_{1}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T}.
Hence (iii) of Proposition
5.1
holds for
\{\xi_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j}
in the case that
j=1.

Recursive step:
Suppose the entities in equations (
4.33
) have been constructed and have the stated properties. Since by the inductive hypothesis
T_{j}
is a compact operator,
there exist

v_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad w_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

such that

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j},\overline{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\overline{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\overline{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j})

is a Schmidt pair for
T_{j}
corresponding to
\|T_{j}\|=t_{j}.
Let us first prove that
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}
is an element of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
By hypothesis,

x_{i}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\dots-\xi_{i-1}\xi_{i-1}^{*})v_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\xi_{i}=\frac{x_{i}}{h_{i}}

for
i=0,\dots,j-1.
Then, for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

\displaystyle\left(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\right)(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\frac{x_{1}}{h_{1}}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\frac{x_{j-1}}{h_{j-1}}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\right)(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j-1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}x_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\right)(z).

We obtain

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j})(z)=\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j-1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}v_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\right)(z),\;\text{for all}\;z\in\mathbb{D},

due to pointwise linear dependence of
\xi_{k}
and

z\mapsto\xi_{k}(z)\langle v_{j}(z),\xi_{k}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

on
\mathbb{D},
for all
k=0,\dots,j-1.
By Proposition
3.22
,

\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j-1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}v_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}

is analytic on
\mathbb{D}.
Moreover, by Proposition
3.26
, since
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{j-1}
are pointwise orthogonal on
\mathbb{T},

\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n})}<\infty.

Therefore

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Let
h_{j}
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{2}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}.
We define

x_{j}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\cdots-\xi_{j-1}\xi_{j-1}^{*})v_{j}

(5.7)

and

\xi_{j}=\frac{x_{j}}{h_{j}}.

(5.8)

Let us show that
\{\xi_{0}(z),\cdots,\xi_{j-1}(z),\xi_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

We have

\xi_{j}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}}v_{j}-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}v_{j}-\cdots-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{j-1}\xi_{j-1}^{*}v_{j},

and so, for
i=0,\dots,j-1,

\begin{array}[]{cll}&\langle\xi_{j}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\xi_{i}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\xi_{i}^{*}(z)\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}^{*}(z)v_{1}(z)-\cdots\\
&\hskip 159.30505pt-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\xi_{i}^{*}(z)\xi_{j-1}(z)\xi_{j-1}^{*}(z)v_{1}(z)\end{array}

almost everywhere on

\mathbb{T}.

Note that by the inductive hypothesis, for
i,k=0,1,\cdots,j-1
and for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T}
,

\xi_{i}^{*}(z)\xi_{k}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,\quad\text{for}\;i\neq k\\
1,\quad\text{for}\;i=k\end{array}.\right.

Thus, for
i=0,\dots,j-1,

\langle\xi_{j}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\xi_{i}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\xi_{i}^{*}(z)\xi_{i}(z)\xi_{i}^{*}v_{j}(z)=0

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
and hence, by induction on
j
and for all integers
j=0,\dots,r-1,

\{\xi_{0}(z),\cdots,\xi_{j-1}(z),\xi_{j}(z)\}
is an orthogonal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T}.

Let us show that

\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge v_{j}(z)=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge x_{j}(z)

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Equation (
5.7
) yields

\begin{array}[]{cllll}&\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge v_{j}(z)\\
&=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge(x_{j}(z)+\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)\\
&\hskip 137.77734pt+\cdots+\xi_{j-1}(z)\xi_{j-1}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z))\\
&=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge(x_{j}(z)+\xi_{0}(z)\langle v_{j}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}+\cdots\\
&\hskip 137.77734pt+\cdots+\xi_{j-1}(z)\langle v_{j}(z),\xi_{j-1}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}})\end{array}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Notice that, for
i=0,\cdots,j-1,

the vectors
\xi_{i}
and
z\mapsto\xi_{i}(z)\langle v_{j}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
are pointwise linearly dependent almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Thus for all
i=0,\cdots,j-1,

\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge\xi_{i}(z)\langle v_{i+1}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=0

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Hence

\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge v_{j}(z)=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge x_{j}(z)\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(5.9)

Next, we shall show that
\|\xi_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=1
for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T}.
Recall that
h_{j}
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{2}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j},
and therefore

\begin{array}[]{cllll}|h_{j}(z)|&=\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge v_{j}(z)\|_{\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge x_{j}(z)\|_{\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}}\\
\end{array}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

By the inductive hypothesis,
\{\xi_{0}(z),\cdots,\xi_{j-1}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},
hence, by Lemma
3.14
,

\displaystyle|h_{j}(z)|

\displaystyle=\|\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge x_{j}(z)\|_{\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}}\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\|x_{j}(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\langle x_{j}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle\xi_{i}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\|x_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(5.10)

Thus

\|\xi_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\frac{\|x_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}}{|h_{j}(z)|}=1

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
and hence, by induction on
j,

\{\xi_{0}(z),\cdots,\xi_{j-1}(z),\xi_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},
and for all integers
j=0,\dots,r-1.

Next, we will prove inductively that the set
\{\bar{\eta}_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j},
defined in equations (
4.41
), is orthonormal. For
i=0,
by equation (
5.1
), we have

\|\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1\;\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

(5.11)

Let
T_{1}
be given by equation (
4.24
).
T_{1}
is assumed to be a compact operator, and if
T_{1}\neq 0,
there exist
v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
w_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that
(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}w_{1})
is a Schmidt pair corresponding to
\|T_{1}\|=t_{1}.
Suppose
h_{1}
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}.
Let

y_{1}=(I_{m}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T})w_{1}=w_{1}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}w_{1}

(5.12)

and let

\eta_{1}(z)=\frac{\bar{z}\bar{y}_{1}(z)}{h_{1}(z)}\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

Then,

\bar{\eta}_{1}(z)=\frac{zy_{0}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}=\frac{zw_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}-\frac{z\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

By equation (
5.11
),
\left\|\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Hence

\begin{array}[]{cllllll}\langle\bar{\eta}_{1}(z),\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&=\eta_{0}^{T}(z)\bar{\eta}_{1}(z)\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}-\frac{z\eta_{0}^{T}(z)\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}-\frac{z\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}-\frac{z\eta_{0}^{T}(z)w_{1}(z)}{\bar{h}_{1}(z)}\\
&=0\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.\par\end{array}

Recall that
h_{1}
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}.

By equation (
5.6
) and Proposition
9.13
,

\|x_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|y_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=|h_{1}(z)|

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
thus

\|\bar{\eta}_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\frac{\|zy_{1}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}}{|\bar{h}_{1}(z)|}=1\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}.

Consequently,
\{\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\bar{\eta}_{1}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T}.
Hence (iii) of Proposition
5.1
holds for
\{\bar{\eta}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{j}
in the case
j=1.

Recursive step:
Suppose the entities in equations (
4.33
) have been constructed and have the stated properties. Since by the inductive hypothesis
T_{j}
is a compact operator,
there exist

v_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad w_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

such that

(\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{2}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j})

is a Schmidt pair for
T_{j}
corresponding to
\|T_{j}\|=t_{j}.
We have proved above that

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Let
h_{j}
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}.
We define

y_{j}=(I_{m}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}-\cdots-\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\eta_{j-1}^{T})w_{j}

and

\bar{\eta}_{j}=\frac{zy_{j}}{\bar{h}_{j}}

(5.13)

Let us show that
\{\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\dots,\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

We have

\bar{\eta}_{j}=\frac{zw_{j}}{\bar{h}_{j}}-\cdots-\frac{{z}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\eta_{j-1}^{T}w_{j}}{\bar{h}_{j}}

and so, for
i=0,\dots,j-1,

\begin{array}[]{cllllll}\langle\bar{\eta}_{j}(z),\bar{\eta}_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&=\eta_{i}^{T}(z)\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{i}^{T}(z)w_{j}(z)}{\bar{h}_{j}(z)}-\cdots-\frac{z\eta_{i}^{T}(z)\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\eta_{j}^{T}(z)w_{j}(z)}{\bar{h}_{j}(z)}\end{array}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Notice that, by the inductive hypothesis, for
i,k=0,\dots,j-1
and for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},

\eta_{i}^{T}(z)\bar{\eta}_{k}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,\quad\text{for}\;i\neq k\\
1,\quad\text{for}\;i=k\end{array}.\right.

Hence, for
i=0,\dots,j-1,

\langle\bar{\eta}_{j}(z),\bar{\eta}_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{i}^{T}(z)w_{j}(z)}{\bar{h}_{j}(z)}-\displaystyle\frac{z\eta_{i}^{T}(z)w_{j}(z)}{\bar{h}_{j}(z)}=0

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Thus by induction on
j
, for all integers
j=0,\dots,r-1,

\{\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\dots,\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\}
is an orthogonal set in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

To complete the proof, we have to prove that

\left\|\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1

for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T}.
Recall that
h_{j}
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}.
By Proposition
10.13
,

|h_{j}(z)|=\|x_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=\|y_{j}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
thus

\left\|\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\right\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=\displaystyle\|\frac{zy_{j}(z)}{\bar{h}_{j}(z)}\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
and hence,
\{\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\dots,\bar{\eta}_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Note that, for
j=1,\dots,r-1
,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}y_{j}

\displaystyle=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(I_{m}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}-\dots-\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\eta_{j-1}^{T})w_{j}\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j}-\sum\limits_{k=0}^{j-1}\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{k}\eta_{k}^{T}w_{j}\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j}

(5.14)

on account of the pointwise linear dependence of
\bar{\eta}_{k}
and
z\mapsto\bar{\eta}_{k}(z)\langle w_{j}(z),\bar{\eta}_{k}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}
.
∎

6.
The closed subspace
X_{j+1}
of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})

Notice that, although
x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
\xi_{0}
is inner,
x_{i}
and
\xi_{i}
might not be in
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
in general for
i=1,\cdots,\min(m,n)-1.
However, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
the pointwise wedge product

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x

is
an element of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})
as the following proposition asserts.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}),
and let
j\leq n-1.
Let the vector-valued functions
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{j}
be constructed after applying steps
0,\dots,j
of the algorithm above and be given by equations (
4.41
). Then

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})

is a subset of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Proof.

For
j=0,
since
G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}),
the Hankel operator
H_{G}
is compact. There exist
x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),y_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that
(x_{0},y_{0})
is a Schmidt pair for the Hankel operator
H_{G}
corresponding to the singular value
\|H_{G}\|.
By Lemma
\ref{2.2},

x_{0},y_{0}
admit the inner-outer factorizations

x_{0}=\xi_{0}h_{0},\quad\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}=\eta_{0}h_{0}

for some inner
\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\eta_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and some scalar outer
h_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}).

Then, by Proposition
3.24
,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Let us now consider the case where
j=1.
By definition,

X_{1}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad Y_{1}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

and, by the inductive hypothesis,
T_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y_{1}
given by equation (
4.24
) is a compact operator. Suppose
\|T_{1}\|\neq 0
and let
(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1},\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}w_{1})
be a Schmidt pair corresponding to
\|T_{1}\|,
where
v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
w_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.
We define

x_{1}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*})v_{1}.

Note that, by Proposition
3.24
,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Let
h_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Then we define

\xi_{1}=\frac{x_{1}}{h_{1}}.

Note that
\xi_{0}
and

z\mapsto\xi_{0}(z)\langle v_{1}(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

are pointwise linearly dependent on
\mathbb{D},
since
\xi_{0}^{*}v_{1}
is a mapping from
\mathbb{D}
to
\mathbb{C}.
Thus, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
z\in\mathbb{D},
we have

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}x)(z)=\displaystyle\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\xi_{1}(z)\wedge x(z)\vskip 8.61108pt\\
=\displaystyle\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\frac{x_{1}(z)}{h_{1}(z)}\wedge x(z),

and by substituting the value of
x_{1}
, we find

\begin{array}[]{cllll}&\displaystyle\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\frac{x_{1}(z)}{h_{1}(z)}\wedge x(z)\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)\wedge(v_{1}(z)-\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z))\wedge x(z)\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)\wedge v_{1}(z)\wedge x(z)-\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}(z)}\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)^{*}v_{1}(z)\wedge x(z)\\
&=\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}x\right)(z).\end{array}

Note that
v_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
h_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
is the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}
. By Proposition
3.22
, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\frac{1}{h_{1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}x

is analytic on
\mathbb{D}.
By Proposition
3.26
, since
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
are pointwise orthogonal almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},

\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}x\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{n})}<\infty.

Hence,

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}x\in\frac{1}{h_{1}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Recursive step:
suppose we have constructed vector-valued functions
\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{j-1},

\eta_{0},\dots,\eta_{j-1},
spaces
X_{j},Y_{j}
and a compact operator
T_{j}\colon X_{j}\to Y_{j}
after applying steps
0,\dots,j
of the algorithm from Subsection
4.2
satisfying

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(6.1)

Since
T_{j}
is a compact operator, there exist vector-valued functions

v_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),w_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j},\overline{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\overline{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}w_{j})

is a Schmidt pair for
T_{j}
corresponding to
\|T_{j}\|.

Define

x_{j}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\dots-\xi_{j-1}\xi_{j-1}^{*})v_{j}.

(6.2)

By assumption,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}
lies in
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Let
h_{j}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
be the scalar outer factor of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}.
Define
\xi_{j}=\frac{x_{j}}{h_{j}}.
Note that

\xi_{i}(z)
and
z\mapsto\xi_{i}(z)\langle v_{j}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}

are pointwise linearly dependent on
\mathbb{D}
for
i=0,\dots,j-1.
Thus, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and all
z\in\mathbb{D},

\displaystyle(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x)(z)

\displaystyle=(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\frac{x_{j}}{h_{j}}\dot{\wedge}x)(z)\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}\bigg(v_{j}(z)-\xi_{0}(z)\xi_{0}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)-\cdots\bigg.

\displaystyle\hskip 129.16626pt-\bigg.\xi_{j-1}(z)\xi_{j-1}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)\bigg)\wedge x(z)\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge\frac{1}{h_{j}(z)}v_{j}(z)\wedge x(z)

\displaystyle-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\dots\wedge\xi_{j-1}(z)\wedge\xi_{i}(z)\xi_{i}^{*}(z)v_{j}(z)\wedge x(z)\vskip 8.61108pt

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\dot{\wedge}x\right)(z).

(6.3)

Recall that, for
i=0,\dots,j-1,
by the algorithm from Subsection
3.2.1,

x_{i}=(I_{n}-\xi_{0}\xi_{0}^{*}-\dots-\xi_{i-1}\xi_{i-1}^{*})v_{i}

and

\xi_{i}=\frac{x_{i}}{h_{i}}.

By equation (
6.3
), for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x)(z)=\left(\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\dot{\wedge}x\right)(z).

Substituting
\frac{x_{i}}{h_{i}}
for
\xi_{i}
in the latter equation, where
x_{i}
are given by equation (
6.2
) for
i=1,\dots,j-1,
we obtain

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x)(z)=\left(\frac{1}{h_{1}}\frac{1}{h_{2}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}v_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\dot{\wedge}x\right)(z),\;z\in\mathbb{D}

on account of the pointwise linear dependence of
\xi_{k}
and

z\mapsto\langle v_{k}(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{k}(z)

on
\mathbb{D},
for
k=0,\dots,j.
By Proposition
3.22
, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\frac{1}{h_{1}}\frac{1}{h_{2}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j}}\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}v_{j}\dot{\wedge}x

is analytic on
\mathbb{D}.
By Proposition
3.26
, since
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{j}
are pointwise orthogonal on
\mathbb{T},

\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})}<\infty.

Thus, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})

and the claim has been proved.

∎

Proposition 6.2
.

In the notation of Proposition
6.1
,

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})

is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Proof.

Let us first show that
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Observe that, by Proposition
3.24
,
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Let

\Xi_{0}=\{f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}):\langle f(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=0\quad\text{almost everywhere on}\;\mathbb{T}\}.

Consider a vector-valued function
w\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).
For all
z\in\mathbb{D},
we may write
w
as

w(z)=w(z)-\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)+\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z).

Then, for all
w\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

\begin{array}[]{clllll}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}w)(z)&=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\big(w(z)-\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)+\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)\big)\\
&=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\big(w(z)-\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)\big)\end{array}

on account of the the pointwise linear dependence of
\xi_{0}
and

z\mapsto\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)
on
\mathbb{D}.

Note that

w(z)-\langle w(z),\xi_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{0}(z)\in\Xi_{0},

thus

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}.

By Corollary
3.32
,
\Xi_{0}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
hence

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\supset\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0},

and so,

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}.

Consider the mapping

C_{\xi_{0}}\colon\Xi_{0}\to\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}

given by

C_{\xi_{0}}w=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}w

for all
w\in\Xi_{0}.
Notice that, by Proposition
5.1
,
\|\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}=1
for almost every
e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{T}.
Therefore, for any
w\in\Xi_{0},
we have

\begin{array}[]{cllllll}\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n})}^{2}&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}w,\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}w\rangle(e^{i\theta})d\theta\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\|\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}\|w(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}-|\langle w(e^{i\theta}),\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle|^{2}\right)\;d\theta\\
&=\|w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})}^{2},\end{array}

since
w
is pointwise orthogonal to
\xi_{0}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Thus the mapping

C_{\xi_{0}}\colon\Xi_{0}\to\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}

is an isometry. Furthermore,
C_{\xi_{0}}\colon\Xi_{0}\to\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}
is a surjective mapping,
thus
\Xi_{0}
and
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}
are isometrically isomorphic. Therefore, since
\Xi_{0}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
the space
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{0}
is complete, hence is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Hence
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

To prove that

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})
, let us consider

\Xi_{j}=\{f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}):\langle f(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}=0,\;\text{for}\;i=0,\cdots,j\}

which is the pointwise orthogonal complement of
\xi_{0},\dots,\xi_{j}
in
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Let
\psi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).
We may write
\psi
as

\psi(z)=\psi(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle\psi(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{i}(z)+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle\psi(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{i}(z).

Then, for all
\psi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\psi)(z)=\xi_{0}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\left(\psi(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle\psi(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{i}(z)\right)

due to the pointwise linear dependence of
\xi_{k}
and

z\mapsto\xi_{k}(z)\langle\psi(z),\xi_{k}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
almost everywhere
on
\mathbb{T}.

Notice that
\left(\psi(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle\psi(z),\xi_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\xi_{i}(z)\right)
is in
\Xi_{j},
thus

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}.

The reverse inclusion holds by the definition of
\Xi_{j},
hence

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}.

Consequently, in order to prove the proposition it suffices to show that
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

By Corollary
3.32
,
\Xi_{j}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
being an intersection of closed subspaces. For any
f\in\Xi_{j},

\begin{array}[]{clllll}&\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})}^{2}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}\|\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}&\cdots&\cdots&\langle\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta}),f(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\\
\langle\xi_{1}(e^{i\theta}),\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}&\|\xi_{1}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}&\cdots&\langle\xi_{1}(e^{i\theta}),f(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\langle f(e^{i\theta}),\xi_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}&\cdots&\cdots&\|f(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}d\theta.\end{array}

Note that
f
and
\xi_{i}
are pointwise orthogonal almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T},
and, by Proposition
5.1
,
\{\xi_{0}(z),\dots,\xi_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T}.

Hence

\begin{array}[]{clll}&\|\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n})}^{2}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\|f(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}d\theta\\
&=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n})}^{2}.\end{array}

Thus

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\cdot\colon\Xi_{j}\to\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}

is an isometry.
Furthermore

(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\cdot)\colon\Xi_{j}\to\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}

is a surjective mapping, thus
\Xi_{j}
and
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}
are isometrically isomorphic. Therefore, since
\Xi_{j}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
the space
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}\Xi_{j}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).
Hence

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})

is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{n}).

∎

7.
The closed subspace
Y_{j+1}
of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

Proposition 7.1
.

Given
\bar{\eta}_{0}=\frac{zy_{0}}{\overline{h_{0}}}
as constructed in the algorithm in Subsection
4.2
the space

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Proof.

As in Proposition
6.1
, one can show that

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\subset zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})

and therefore

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\overline{H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})}\subset\bar{z}\overline{H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}.

Hence

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

By virtue of the fact that complex conjugation is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
an equivalent statement to Proposition
7.1
is that
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).
Let

V=\{f\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\;:\;\langle f(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=0\quad\text{for almost all}\;z\in\mathbb{T}\}

be the pointwise orthogonal complement of
\eta_{0}
in
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Consider
g\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).
We may write
g
as

g(z)=g(z)-\langle g(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\cdot\eta_{0}(z)+\langle g(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\cdot\eta_{0}(z)

for every
z\in\mathbb{D}.
Then, for all
g\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

(\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}g)(z)=\eta_{0}(z)\wedge[g(z)-\langle g(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\eta_{0}(z)]

on account of the pointwise linear dependence of
\eta_{0}
and
z\mapsto\langle g(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\eta_{0}(z)
on
\mathbb{D}.

Note that
g(z)-\langle g(z),\eta_{0}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\eta_{0}(z)\in V,

thus

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\subset\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V.

The reverse inclusion is obvious, hence

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})=\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V.

To prove the proposition, it suffices to show that
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Consider the mapping

C_{\eta_{0}}\colon V\to\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V

defined by

C_{\eta_{0}}\nu=\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\nu

for all
\nu\in V.
Notice that, by Proposition
5.1
,
\|\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}=1
for almost every
e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{T}.
Then, for any
\upsilon\in V,
we have

\begin{array}[]{cllllll}\|\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\upsilon\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}^{2}&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\upsilon,\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\upsilon\rangle(e^{i\theta})d\theta\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\|\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\|\upsilon(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}-|\langle\upsilon(e^{i\theta}),\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle|^{2}\right)\;d\theta\\
&=\|\upsilon\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})}^{2},\end{array}

since

\upsilon
is pointwise orthogonal to
\eta_{0}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Thus the mapping
C_{\eta_{0}}\colon V\to\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V
is an isometry.

Note that by Corollary
3.32
,
V
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).
Furthermore,

C_{\eta_{0}}\colon V\to\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V

is a surjective mapping,
thus
V
and
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V
are isometrically isomorphic. Therefore, since
V
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
the space
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}V
is complete and therefore a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Hence
\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
is complete and therefore a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

∎

Corollary 7.2
.

The orthogonal projection
P_{Y_{1}}
from
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
onto
\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
is well defined.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.30
,
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
can be identified with a closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}),
thus we have

H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})\ominus H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Now the assertion follows immediately from Proposition
7.1
.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
0\leq j\leq m-2.
Let the functions
\bar{\eta}_{i}
be given by equations (
4.41
) in the algorithm from Subsection
4.2
, that is,
\bar{\eta}_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{zy_{i}}{\overline{h}_{i}}
for
i=0,\cdots,j.
Then, the space

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

is a closed linear subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Proof.

First let us show that, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zx\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Recall that

y_{j}=(I_{m}-\bar{\eta}_{0}\eta_{0}^{T}-\dots-\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\eta_{j-1}^{T})w_{j}

and

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{y}_{j}=\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(\bar{z}\bar{w}_{j}-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j-1}{\eta}_{i}\eta_{i}^{*}\bar{z}\bar{w}_{j})=\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w}_{j}

(7.1)

because of the pointwise linear dependence of
\eta_{i}
and

z\mapsto\langle\bar{z}\bar{w}_{j+1}(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\eta_{i}(z)
on
\mathbb{D}.

By Proposition
10.13
,

|h_{i}(z)|=\|y_{i}(z)\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}

almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Substituting
\eta_{i}=\frac{\bar{z}\bar{y}_{i}}{h_{i}}
for all
i=0,\dots,j-1
in equation (
7.1
), we obtain

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{y}_{j}=\frac{1}{h_{0}}\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j}}\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w_{1}}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w_{j}}.

Observe that, by Proposition
3.22
, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\frac{1}{h_{0}}\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j}}\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w_{1}}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w_{j}}\dot{\wedge}zx

is analytic on
\mathbb{D}.
By Proposition
3.26
, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
since
\eta_{0},\cdots,\eta_{j}
are pointwise orthogonal on
\mathbb{T},

\|\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zx\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}<\infty.

Hence, for every
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zx=z\frac{1}{h_{0}}\frac{1}{h_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{h_{j}}\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{z}\bar{w}_{j}\dot{\wedge}x

is in
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Taking complex conjugates, we infer that

Y_{j+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Let us prove that
Y_{j+1}
is a closed linear subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.
Since complex conjugation is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
an equivalent statement to the above is that

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})

is a closed linear subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Let

V_{j}=\{\varphi\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\;:\;\langle\varphi(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=0,\;\text{for}\;i=0,\cdots,j\}

be the pointwise orthogonal complement of

\eta_{0},\cdots,\eta_{j}
in
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).
Consider
f\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}).
We may write
f
as

f(z)=f(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle f(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle\eta_{i}(z)+\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle f(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle\eta_{i}(z).

Then, for all
f\in zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},

(\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}f)(z)=\eta_{0}(z)\wedge\eta_{1}(z)\wedge\cdots\wedge\eta_{j}(z)\wedge\left(f(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle f(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle\eta_{i}(z)\right).

Notice that
\left(f(z)-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{j}\langle f(z),\eta_{i}(z)\rangle\eta_{i}(z)\right)\in V_{j},
thus

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})\subset\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}.

The reverse inclusion holds by the definition of
V_{j},

hence

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})=\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}.

Consequently, in order to prove the proposition it suffices to show that
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

By Corollary
3.32
,
V_{j}
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
being an intersection of closed subspaces.
For any
f\in V_{j},
we have

\begin{array}[]{clllll}&\|\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}^{2}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}\|\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}&\cdots&\langle\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta}),f(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\langle\eta_{1}(e^{i\theta}),\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\|\eta_{1}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}&\cdots\\
\vdots&&\hskip-86.11084pt\ddots\\
\langle f(e^{i\theta}),\eta_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\cdots&\|f(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}d\theta.\end{array}

Note that
f
and
\eta_{i}
are pointwise orthogonal almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}
and, by Proposition
5.1
,
\{\eta_{0}(z),\dots,\eta_{j}(z)\}
is an orthonormal set for almost every
z\in\mathbb{T}.

Hence

\begin{array}[]{clll}&\|\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}^{2}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\|f(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\end{pmatrix}d\theta\\
&=\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})}^{2}.\end{array}

Thus

\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}\cdot\colon V_{j}\to\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}

is an isometry.
Furthermore

(\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}\cdot)\colon V_{j}\to\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\eta_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}

is a surjective mapping, thus
V_{j}
and
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}
are isometrically isomorphic. Therefore, since
V_{j}
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
the space
\eta_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\eta_{j}\dot{\wedge}V_{j}
is a closed subspace of
zH^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).
Hence

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

is a closed subspace of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

∎

Corollary 7.4
.

Let
0\leq j\leq m-2.

The orthogonal projection

P_{Y_{j}}\colon L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})\to Y_{j}

is well-defined.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.30
,
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})
can be identified with a closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}),
thus we have

H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}=L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m})\ominus H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{j+2}\mathbb{C}^{m}).

Now the assertion follows immediately from Proposition
7.3
.
∎

8.
T_{j}
is a well-defined operator

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
and let
0\leq j\leq\min(m,n)-2.
Let the functions
\xi_{i},\eta_{i}
be defined by equations (
4.41
), that is,

\xi_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{x_{i}}{h_{i}},\quad\eta_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{\bar{z}\bar{\eta_{i}}}{h_{i}}

(8.1)

for
i=0,\cdots,j
and let

X_{i}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{i-1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{i+1}\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad i=0,\cdots,j,

Y_{i}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{i-1}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{i+1}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp},\quad i=0,\cdots,j.

Let
Q_{i}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfy

(G-Q_{i})x_{k}=t_{k}y_{k},\quad(G-Q_{i})^{*}y_{k}=t_{k}x_{k}

(8.2)

for
k=0,\dots,i-1.

Then, the operators
T_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Y_{i},

i=0,\cdots,j,
given by

T_{i}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{i-1}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{i}}\left(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{i-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{i})x\right)

(8.3)

are well-defined and are independent of the choice of
Q_{i}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfying equations (
8.2
).

Proof.

By Corollary
7.4
, the projections
P_{Y_{i}}
are well-defined for all
i=0,\cdots,j.

Hence it suffices to show that, for all
i=0,1,\cdots,j,

T_{i}
maps a zero from its domain to a zero in its range and that
T_{i}
does not depend on the choice of
Q_{i},
which satisfies equations (
8.2
).

For
i=0,
the operator
T_{0}
is the Hankel operator
H_{G}.
If
f_{0}\equiv 0,
then
H_{G}f_{0}=0
and, moreover,
H_{G}
is independent of the choice of any
Q\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
as
H_{G-Q}=H_{G}.
Thus,
T_{0}
is well-defined.

For
i=1,
let
(x_{0},y_{0})
be a Schmidt pair for the compact operator
H_{G}
corresponding to
t_{0}=\|H_{G}\|,
where
x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
y_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

By Lemma
4.12
,
x_{0},\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}
admit the inner-outer factorisations
x_{0}=\xi_{0}h_{0},\quad\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}=\eta_{0}h_{0},
where
\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

\eta_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
are inner vector-valued functions and
h_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
is scalar outer. The spaces
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
are given by the formulas

X_{1}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad Y_{1}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

The operator
T_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y_{1}
is given by

T_{1}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)

for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
where
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfies equations (
8.2
).

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}u=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v
for some
u,v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Then

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})u=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})v.

Proof.

Suppose that
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}u=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}v
for some
u,v\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Let
x=u-v
, then
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x=0
, and so
x
and
\xi_{0}
are pointwise linearly dependent in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
on
\mathbb{D}.

Therefore there exist maps
\beta,\lambda\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{C}
, having no common zero in
\mathbb{D}
,
such that

\beta(z)\xi_{0}(z)=\lambda(z)x(z)\;\;\text{in}\;\mathbb{C}^{n},

(8.4)

for all
z\in\mathbb{D}.
By assumption,
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfies equations (
8.2
). Thus, for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

t_{0}y_{0}(z)=(G-Q_{1})(z)x_{0}(z).

(8.5)

By equations (
8.1
) and (
8.4
),

\beta(z)x_{0}(z)=\beta(z)h_{0}(z)\xi_{0}(z)=h_{0}(z)\lambda(z)x(z)

(8.6)

for all
z\in\mathbb{D}.
By equations (
8.5
) and (
8.6
), for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

\begin{array}[]{cllll}t_{0}y_{0}(z)&=(G-Q_{1})(z)x_{0}(z),\\
\beta(z)t_{0}z\displaystyle\frac{y_{0}(z)}{\bar{h}_{0}(z)}&=\displaystyle(G-Q_{1})(z)h_{0}(z)\lambda(z)x(z)\frac{z}{\bar{h}_{0}(z)}.\end{array}

Therefore, by equations (
8.1
), for all
z\in\mathbb{D},

t_{0}\beta(z)\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)=(G-Q_{1})(z)x(z)\mu(z)\;\;\text{in}\;\mathbb{C}^{m},

where

\mu(z)=\frac{zh_{0}(z)\lambda(z)}{\bar{h}_{0}(z)},\;\text{for all }\;z\in\mathbb{D}.

Hence, by Definition
3.20
,
\bar{\eta}_{0}
and
(G-Q_{1})x
are pointwise linearly dependent in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
on
\mathbb{D},
and so

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x=0.

Consequently,

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})u=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})v.

∎

Therefore the formula (
8.3
) (with
i=1
) does uniquely define
T_{1}u\in Y_{1}
.
Next, we show that the operator
T_{1}
is independent of the choice of
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfying equations (
8.2
).

Suppose
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfy

(G-Q_{1})x_{0}=t_{0}y_{0}\;,\;y_{0}^{*}(G-Q_{1})=t_{0}x_{0}^{*}

(8.7)

and

(G-Q_{2})x_{0}=t_{0}y_{0}\;,\;y_{0}^{*}(G-Q_{2})=t_{0}x_{0}^{*}.

(8.8)

Then, we claim that, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)=P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{2})x),

that is,

P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{1}-Q_{2})x)=0.

The latter equation is equivalent to the statement that, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
,

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x
is orthogonal to
Y_{1}
, that is, to
\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\varrho
for all

\varrho\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.
As a matter of convenience, set

Ax=(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x,\quad x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).

We have to prove that

\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}Ax,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\varrho\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}=0

for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and all
\varrho\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Note that

\begin{array}[]{clllllll}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}Ax,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\varrho\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\dot{\wedge}A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\dot{\wedge}\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;d\theta,\end{array}

which by Proposition
3.12
yields

\begin{array}[]{llll}&\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\end{pmatrix}d\theta\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\|\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}^{2}\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;d\theta\\
&\hskip 21.52771pt-\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\;d\theta.\end{array}

By Proposition
5.1
,
\|\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\|_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}=1
for almost every
e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{T}.
Since
Ax\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
\varrho\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta}\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}d\theta=\langle Ax,\varrho\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})}=0.

Thus

\begin{array}[]{cllllll}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}Ax,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\varrho\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}d\theta\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{\eta}_{0}^{*}(e^{i\theta})A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta})\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}d\theta.\end{array}

Recall that by equation (
4.17
),
\bar{\eta}_{0}(z)=\displaystyle\frac{zy_{0}(z)}{\bar{h}_{0}(z)},\;z\in\mathbb{T},
so that

\bar{\eta}_{0}^{*}(e^{i\theta})=\left(\frac{e^{i\theta}y_{0}(e^{i\theta})}{\bar{h}_{0}(e^{i\theta})}\right)^{*}=\frac{e^{-i\theta}y_{0}^{*}(e^{i\theta})}{h_{0}(e^{i\theta})}.

Therefore

\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}Ax,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\varrho\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{m})}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{e^{-i\theta}y_{0}^{*}(e^{i\theta})}{h_{0}(e^{i\theta})}A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta})\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}d\theta.

Recall our
initial assumption was that
Q_{1},Q_{2}
satisfy equations (
8.7
) and (
8.8
), consequently,

y_{0}^{*}(G-Q_{i})=t_{0}x_{0}^{*},\;\text{for}\;i=1,2.

Hence, for
z\in\mathbb{T},

\begin{array}[]{clll}y_{0}^{*}(z)A(z)x(z)&=y_{0}^{*}(z)(G-Q_{1})(z)x(z)-y_{0}^{*}(z)(G-Q_{2})(z)x(z)\\
&=(t_{0}x_{0}^{*}x-t_{0}x_{0}^{*}x)(z)\\
&=0.\end{array}

We deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\bar{\eta}_{0}^{*}(e^{i\theta})A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta})\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\varrho(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}d\theta=0.

To conclude, we have proved that, if
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfy equations (
8.7
) and (
8.8
), then

P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)=P_{Y_{1}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{2})x),

that is,
T_{1}
is independent of the choice of
Q_{1}
subject to equations (
8.7
),(
8.8
).

Recursive step: suppose that functions
x_{i-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

y_{i-1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m}),
outer functions
h_{i-1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}),
positive numbers
t_{i},
matrix-valued functions
Q_{i}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}),
spaces
X_{i},Y_{i}
and compact operators
T_{i}\colon X_{i}\to Y_{i}
are constructed inductively by the algorithm for
i=0,\dots,j.

Let us prove that
T_{j}\colon X_{j}\to Y_{j},
given by equation (
4.37
), is well-defined for
0\leq j\leq r
. Note, by Corollary
7.4
, the projection
P_{Y_{j}}
is well-defined.

We must show that if an element of
X_{j}
has two different expressions as an element of
\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
say

u=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}x=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}\tilde{x}

(8.9)

for some
x,\tilde{x}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),
then the two corresponding formulae for
T_{j}u
given by the defining equation (
4.37
) agree, that is,

P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j})x)=P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j})\tilde{x}),

or equivalently,

P_{Y_{j}}\bigg(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})\bigg)=0,

which is to say that we need to show that

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{1}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})\in Y_{j}^{\perp}.

(8.10)

If
x,\tilde{x}
satisfy equation (
8.9
), then

\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\xi_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(x-\tilde{x})=0,

and so, by Corollary
3.13
,
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{j-1},x-\tilde{x}
are pointwise linearly dependent almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

It follows immediately that, for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},
the vectors

(G-Q_{j})\xi_{0}(z),\dots,(G-Q_{j})\xi_{j-1}(z),(G-Q_{j})\xi_{j}(z),(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})(z)

are linearly dependent in
\mathbb{C}^{m}.

Since
y_{j}=\bar{z}\bar{h}_{j}\bar{\eta}_{j},
by equations (
1.19
), equations (
8.2
) imply, for
i=0,\dots,j-1
and almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},

\displaystyle(G-Q_{j})\xi_{i}(z)=(G-Q_{j})\frac{x_{i}}{h_{i}}(z)=t_{i}\frac{y_{i}}{h_{i}}(z)=\frac{t_{i}}{h_{i}}\bar{z}\bar{h}_{i}\bar{\eta}_{i}(z).

Thus, for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T},
the vectors

\frac{t_{0}}{h_{0}}\bar{z}\bar{h}_{0}\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\;\dots,\;\frac{t_{j-1}}{h_{j-1}}\bar{z}\bar{h}_{j-1}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(z),\;(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})(z)

are linearly dependent in
\mathbb{C}^{m}.
Since
t_{0}\geq t_{1}\geq\dots\geq t_{j}>0
,
it follows that

\bar{\eta}_{0}(z),\;\dots,\;\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(z),\;(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})(z)

are linearly dependent for almost all
z\in\mathbb{T}
and so, by Corollary
3.13
,

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\dots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{j})(x-\tilde{x})=0,

which certainly implies the desired relation (
8.10
).
Thus
T_{j}\colon X_{j}\to Y_{j}
is well-defined.

For the operator
T_{j}
to be uniquely defined in the algorithm, it remains to prove

T_{j}
is independent of the choice of
Q_{j}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
subject to equations (
8.2
). Let
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
satisfy

(G-Q_{1})x_{i}=t_{i}y_{i},\quad(G-Q_{2})x_{i}=t_{i}y_{i},\quad y_{i}^{*}(G-Q_{1})=t_{i}x_{i}^{*},\quad y_{i}^{*}(G-Q_{2})=t_{i}x_{i}^{*}

(8.11)

for
i=0,\cdots,j-1.

We shall prove that, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)=P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{2})x).

The latter equality holds if and only if, for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),

P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x)=0

which is equivalent to the assertion that

\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x
is orthogonal to
\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}q
for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and for all

q\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Equivalently

\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}q\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{m})}=0

for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and for all
q\in H^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}.

Set
Ax=(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x,

x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).

By Proposition
3.12
,

\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}q\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{m})}

is equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\footnotesize{\det\begin{pmatrix}\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\cdots&\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
\langle\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\cdots&\langle\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\langle\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\cdots&\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\bar{\eta}_{j-1}(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\end{pmatrix}d\theta}.

Notice that
Ax
and
q
are orthogonal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})
and, by Proposition
5.1
,
\{\bar{\eta}_{i}(z)\}_{i=0}^{j-1}
is an orthonormal sequence in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.
Also, for
i=0,\cdots,j-1,
by equations (
8.11
),

\begin{array}[]{cllll}\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),\overline{\eta}_{i}(e^{i\theta})\rangle\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}&=\displaystyle\eta_{i}^{T}(e^{i\theta})A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta})\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{e^{-i\theta}y_{i}^{*}(e^{i\theta})}{h_{i}(e^{i\theta})}A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta})\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{e^{-i\theta}}{h_{i}(e^{i\theta})}\left(y_{i}^{*}(e^{i\theta})(G-Q_{1})(z)x(z)-y_{i}^{*}(e^{i\theta})(G-Q_{2})(z)x(z)\right)\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{e^{-i\theta}}{h_{i}(e^{i\theta})}(t_{i}x_{i}^{*}x-t_{i}x_{i}^{*}x)=0.\end{array}

Thus

\begin{array}[]{clllllll}&\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}(Q_{2}-Q_{1})x,\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j}\dot{\wedge}q\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\wedge^{j+1}\mathbb{C}^{m})}\\
&=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\limits_{0}^{2\pi}\det\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&\langle\bar{\eta}_{0}(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
0&1&\cdots&\langle\bar{\eta}_{2}(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\langle A(e^{i\theta})x(e^{i\theta}),q(e^{i\theta})\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{m}}\end{pmatrix}\;d\theta\\
&=\langle Ax,q\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m})}\vskip 8.61108pt=0.\end{array}

Consequently

P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x)=P_{Y_{j}}(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdots\dot{\wedge}\bar{\eta}_{j-1}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{2})x),

and so

T_{j}
is independent of the choice of
Q_{j}
subject to equations (
8.2
).
∎

9.
Compactness of the operators
T_{1}
and
T_{2}

Here we use notations from the algorithm of Subsection
4.2
to prove the compactness of the operator
T_{j}
given by equation (
4.37
) for
j=1,2
.
The proof requires several steps. Let us first prove that the operator
T_{1}
is compact.

Recall that since
G\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}),
by Hartman’s theorem, the operator
T_{0}=H_{G}
is compact and hence there exist
x_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
y_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
such that
(x_{0},y_{0})
is a Schmidt pair for
H_{G}
corresponding to the singular value
\|H_{G}\|=t_{0}.

By Lemma
4.12
,
x_{0},\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}
admit the inner-outer factorizations

x_{0}=\xi_{0}h_{0},\quad\bar{z}\bar{y}_{0}=\eta_{0}h_{0},

(9.1)

where
\xi_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})
,
\eta_{0}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})
are vector-valued inner functions and
h_{0}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
is a scalar outer function. Moreover there exist unitary-valued functions of types
n\times n,m\times m
respectively, of the form

V_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{0}&\bar{\alpha}_{0}\end{pmatrix},\quad W_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{0}&\bar{\beta}_{0}\end{pmatrix}^{T},

(9.2)

where
\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}
are inner, co-outer, quasi-continuous functions of types
n\times(n-1)
,
m\times(m-1)
respectively and all minors on the first columns of
V_{0},W_{0}^{T}
are in
H^{\infty}
. Furthermore every
Q_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m\times n})
which is at minimal distance from
G
satisfies

W_{0}(G-Q_{1})V_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}t_{0}u_{0}&0\\
0&F_{1}\end{pmatrix}

for some

F_{1}\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{(m-1)\times(n-1)})+C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{C}^{(m-1)\times(n-1)})

and some quasi-continuous function
u_{0}
with
|u_{0}(z)|=1
almost everywhere on
\mathbb{T}.

Recall that

X_{1}=\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\quad Y_{1}=\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}

and
T_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y_{1}
is given by

T_{1}(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}x)=P_{Y_{1}}[\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}(G-Q_{1})x]\quad\text{for all}\;x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n}).

Our first endeavour in this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let

\mathcal{K}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\emph{def}}}{{=}}V_{0}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n-1})\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{L}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\emph{def}}}{{=}}W_{0}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m-1})^{\perp}\end{pmatrix},

(9.3)

and let the maps

U_{1}\colon H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n-1})\to\mathcal{K}_{1},

U_{2}\colon H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m-1})^{\perp}\to\mathcal{L}_{1}

be given by

U_{1}x=V_{0}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
x\end{pmatrix},\quad U_{2}y=W_{0}^{*}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
y\end{pmatrix}

for all
x\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n-1}),\;y\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m-1})^{\perp}.
Consider the operator
\Gamma_{1}=P_{\mathcal{L}_{1}}M_{G-Q_{1}}|_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}.

Then

(i)

The maps
U_{1},U_{2}
are unitaries.

(ii)

The maps
(\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdot)\colon\mathcal{K}_{1}\to H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
(\bar{\eta}_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdot)\colon\mathcal{L}_{1}\to H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{m})^{\perp}
are unitaries.

(iii)

The following
diagram is commutative:

\begin{array}[]{clllll}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n-1})&\xrightarrow{U_{1}}&\mathcal{K}_{1}&\xrightarrow{\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}\cdot}&\xi_{0}\dot{\wedge}H^{2}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}^{n})=X_{1}\\
